# THE WATER REMEMBERS THE MACHINE

## A 500-source micro-cut performance score

This is a composition, not a playlist and not a chain of ordinary transitions.
Five hundred different tracks each contribute one short, analyzed fragment. The
fragments become a new piece through rhythm, recurrence of timbral ideas,
harmonic implication, contrast, and controlled changes in density.

The DJ must finish the entire score before playback begins. Once the score is
committed, the engine performs it without asking the model what to do next.

## Performance identity

- **Emotional sentence:** wake inside a clean synthetic memory, discover the
  machinery beneath it, let that machinery become ecstatic, then leave with a
  human afterimage rather than a triumphant ending.
- **Duration:** approximately 12 minutes 48 seconds.
- **Sources:** exactly 500 unique, streamable tracks discovered outside the
  listener's likes.
- **Source reuse:** none. A source appears once. Motifs recur through family
  resemblance, not through replaying the same record.
- **Fragment length:** 1, 2, 4, 8, or 16 beats. Never more than 16 beats.
- **Authored fragments:** exactly 500.
- **Silence:** intentional only. Maximum exposed silence is one eighth-note.
- **Primary continuity:** timbre first, rhythm second, harmonic implication
  third. Similar BPM alone is not a reason to place two fragments together.
- **Narrative energy:** fragile → curious → propulsive → overwhelming →
  falsely euphoric → intimate.

## Evidence rule

Every source must have a completed rich analysis before it enters the score.
Each fragment must cite:

1. exact SoundCloud track ID and title;
2. analyzed segment ID;
3. exact source start and end seconds;
4. source BPM, confidence, Camelot key, and key confidence;
5. local energy, energy slope, rhythmic density, vocal probability, valence,
   arousal, and dark/bright timbre;
6. the musical role inferred from that evidence;
7. the score time, duration in beats, bus, gain, EQ, tempo treatment, and
   transition gesture.

Do not infer a fragment from its title, genre tag, artwork, follower count, or
search query. Search creates the crate. Analysis earns a place in the piece.

## Fragment inventory

The distribution is fixed before selection:

| Length | Count | Compositional use |
|---|---:|---|
| 1 beat | 180 | consonants, kick substitutions, vocal splinters, hard edits |
| 2 beats | 140 | answers, syncopations, alternating timbral cells |
| 4 beats | 100 | recognizable gestures, phrase punctuation |
| 8 beats | 60 | local continuity, breakdown windows, false arrivals |
| 16 beats | 20 | movement thresholds and rare moments of trust |
| **Total** | **500** | **one fragment from every source** |

Short does not mean random. A one-beat cut must complete a rhythmic or timbral
sentence. If removing it does not weaken the sentence, remove or replace it.

## Buses

- **A — body:** the fragment currently carrying pulse or weight.
- **B — apparition:** the next fragment, answer, or interruption.
- **C — memory:** filtered tails, spectral smears, and pre-echoes made only
  from already scheduled fragments. C never introduces an uncredited source.
- **D — knife:** beat-accurate one- and two-beat cuts. No reverb tail by
  default; each exception is written into the score.

Only A and B may carry full-band material together. C is always high-passed or
band-limited. D is momentary. The master must retain at least 3 dB of headroom.

## Movement map

| Movement | Score time | Fragments | Pulse | Energy shape | Dramatic job |
|---|---|---:|---|---|---|
| I. Glass Wake | 00:00–01:31 | 48 | free → 90 | 0.10 → 0.34 | Teach the ear the piece has memory |
| II. Waterline | 01:31–02:52 | 64 | 90 → 116 | 0.28 → 0.53 | Turn texture into gait |
| III. Packet Bloom | 02:52–05:07 | 96 | 116 → 132 | 0.45 → 0.72 | Make discontinuity feel inevitable |
| IV. Collision Garden | 05:07–08:22 | 128 | 132 → 164 | 0.62 → 0.98 → 0.76 | Overwhelm without flattening dynamics |
| V. False Heaven | 08:22–10:52 | 96 | 164 → 130 | 0.74 → 0.88 → 0.48 | Offer euphoria, reveal its artificiality |
| VI. Skin of the Screen | 10:52–12:47 | 68 | 130 → free | 0.44 → 0.08 | Return to intimacy without restoring innocence |

Fragment numbers are global. `F001` is the first sound; `F500` is the last.

## Motif grammar

### M1 — water

Bright, low-vocal, low-attack fragments with a stable or gently rising local
energy slope. They arrive high-passed, reveal their body over 2–8 beats, and
leave a narrow spectral trace on C. Water is not “ambient”; it is perceived
continuity between unrelated sources.

### M2 — face

Vocal probability above the surrounding texture, but never enough uninterrupted
language to become a verse. Faces begin as breath and vowel residue, become
command-like splinters in Movement IV, then return as vulnerable consonants in
Movement VI.

### M3 — machine

High rhythmic density, low vocal probability, decisive attacks. The machine
first appears as isolated one-beat substitutions. It eventually carries whole
four-beat cells, but no single source is allowed to become “the beat.”

### M4 — false sun

High-valence fragments whose arousal is lower than their brightness suggests.
They create uplift without certainty. The false sun blooms fully in Movement V
and is immediately contradicted by darker fragments with related pitch content.

### M5 — scar

A dark or noisy one-beat fragment placed at the same relative point in a
four-bar sentence: beat 4-and of bar 4. Its source changes every time. The scar
is the only formally predictable cut in the piece.

## Transition vocabulary

Use these gestures deliberately; never choose one at random.

- **Inheritance:** the next fragment enters with one clearly shared property
  (pitch center, transient shape, vowel, noise color, or pulse subdivision)
  while changing the others.
- **Contradiction:** a fragment opposes the current energy or valence but
  preserves a smaller detail so the cut reads as revelation, not accident.
- **Decapitation:** a stable 4- or 8-beat fragment is cut one beat before its
  expected completion; a D-bus fragment supplies the missing final beat.
- **Stutter ladder:** four different sources contribute `2 + 1 + 1 + 4` beats.
  The attack shortens, then the phrase suddenly breathes.
- **Spectral relay:** outgoing low band, incoming high band, then exchange over
  2–4 beats. Never run two full-band fragments merely because keys match.
- **False drop:** remove the body, retain memory-bus residue, present a
  high-energy one-beat fragment, then refuse the expected sustained drop.
- **Lens flare:** three bright fragments of 1 beat each cross the stereo field;
  the fourth fragment is darker, centered, and 4 beats long.
- **Human cut:** after a machine-dense passage, allow an imperfect or
  low-confidence vocal fragment to enter dry and unquantized by at most 35 ms.

## Movement I — Glass Wake

**Fragments:** `F001–F048`

The first sound is not a beat. It is a narrow, almost anonymous high-frequency
detail from the lowest-energy analyzed fragment in the opening pool. During the
first 24 fragments, no source receives more than 8 beats and no kick transient
may repeat at a stable interval more than twice.

### Phrase design

- `F001–F008`: eight fragments form one impossible inhale. Durations:
  `8, 4, 2, 2, 4, 1, 1, 8` beats in free time. Adjacent fragments inherit
  brightness or noise color.
- `F009–F016`: introduce M2 as nonverbal traces. Keep every vocal fragment
  below -12 dB and remove its low band.
- `F017–F024`: first M5 scar. The listener should notice an interruption, not
  yet a pattern.
- `F025–F032`: imply 90 BPM using alternating 2-beat and 4-beat fragments, but
  withhold a stable kick.
- `F033–F040`: first stutter ladder. The 4-beat landing must be lower in energy
  than its one-beat approach cuts.
- `F041–F048`: a 16-beat threshold assembled from five sources. The last source
  introduces a low pulse that Movement II can inherit.

Do not make this movement uniformly quiet. Its drama comes from detail,
proximity, and the suspicion of a larger mechanism.

## Movement II — Waterline

**Fragments:** `F049–F112`

Establish gait without granting a conventional groove. The pulse moves from
approximately 90 to 116 BPM through subdivision: the same perceived interval
is reinterpreted, not gradually pitch-bent for 95 seconds.

### Phrase design

- Four 16-fragment sentences. Each sentence contains:
  - one 8-beat body fragment;
  - two 4-beat replies;
  - four 2-beat connectors;
  - eight 1-beat micro-cuts;
  - one 4-beat contradictory fragment that previews the next sentence.
- Sentence 1 foregrounds M1.
- Sentence 2 introduces dry hand/percussion transients as M3.
- Sentence 3 lets M2 and M3 answer each other.
- Sentence 4 makes the water motif rhythmic and hands its low-mid pulse to
  Movement III.

The final 16 beats must feel more mobile but not louder than the middle of the
movement.

## Movement III — Packet Bloom

**Fragments:** `F113–F208`

This is where cuts become the groove. Use a 132 BPM score grid while preserving
recognizable source attack shapes. Tempo correction above ±6% is forbidden;
when source tempo disagrees, use half-time, double-time, or non-pitched cuts.

### Phrase design

- Six 16-fragment packets.
- Every packet uses a different ordering of the same duration multiset:
  `8, 4, 4, 2, 2, 2, 2, 1, 1, 1, 1, 1, 1, 1, 1, 4`.
- Packets 1–2: inheritance dominates.
- Packets 3–4: decapitation and stutter ladders dominate.
- Packet 5: three false drops in decreasing gaps.
- Packet 6: reduce the number of simultaneous bands while increasing cut
  frequency. Density rises; loudness does not.

`F208` is an 8-beat fragment with a rising local energy slope and enough
rhythmic certainty to make the acceleration into Movement IV feel chosen.

## Movement IV — Collision Garden

**Fragments:** `F209–F336`

The peak is a garden, not a wall. Maintain separate foreground, interruption,
and memory layers. The pulse accelerates by structural reinterpretation:
132 → 150 → 164 BPM.

### Phrase design

- `F209–F240`: 32-fragment hard-percussion lattice. Each four-beat span uses at
  least three sources. No source supplies kick and snare together.
- `F241–F272`: M2 becomes command-like. Alternate one-beat vocal fragments with
  two-beat machine replies; never allow a full lyric.
- `F273–F304`: maximum cut density. Use 64 physical edits made from 32 credited
  fragments by slicing each analyzed fragment internally at verified beat
  boundaries. Source attribution remains one fragment, not 64 new sources.
- `F305–F320`: first true 16-beat trust window, but it is a composite of four
  sources relayed by frequency band.
- `F321–F336`: peak and recoil. `F329` carries the highest local analyzed energy
  in the entire score. `F330–F336` descend in arousal while preserving pulse.

Never place all high-energy fragments together. The peak requires low-energy
negative space around its most forceful attacks.

### Maximum-density execution contract

For `F273–F304`, every credited fragment is exactly two source beats and
contains exactly two separately gated physical edits, `A` and `B`. The hard-cut
boundary is `cue start + 60/source BPM`; there is no crossfade and no hidden
time stretch. Half-time or double-time language describes perception only.
Thus 32 credited sources produce exactly 64 physical edits while remaining 32
score rows.

### Composite-trust execution contract

For `F305–F320`, form four sequential 16-beat windows. Each window contains
four credited sources that start together and contribute exactly 16 source
beats each. Relay them by frequency band: sub below 140 Hz, low-mid from
140–700 Hz, presence from 700 Hz–4.5 kHz, and air above 4.5 kHz. Time-correct
each source to the window master by no more than ±6%; the master grid determines
the rendered window duration. The four band-limited sources sum to one
composite. They are not four serial tracks, and none may become an unplanned
full-band layer.

## Movement V — False Heaven

**Fragments:** `F337–F432`

Brightness and consonance arrive after the peak, so they initially feel like
release. M4 expands from one-beat flashes into 8- and 16-beat windows. The
movement gradually reveals that the uplift is made from fragments whose
emotional evidence disagrees.

### Phrase design

- `F337–F352`: lens flare repeated four times, each repetition brighter.
- `F353–F384`: euphoric mosaic at 130 BPM. Favor bright timbre and high valence,
  but interleave low-arousal or wistful evidence.
- `F385–F400`: longest harmonic implication in the score. Eight fragments
  outline a compatible tonal path without becoming a conventional key mix.
- `F401–F416`: contradict every bright arrival with a darker fragment sharing
  one pitch or vocal quality.
- `F417–F432`: remove drums in stages. Preserve forward motion using consonant
  attacks and breath spacing.

### Lens-flare execution contract

`F337–F352` contains four consecutive four-fragment cells. Every cell uses
`1/2/4/8` source beats in that order: spark, answer, bloom, afterimage. Within
each cell, cited source brightness must rise strictly. Across cells, mean cited
brightness and the written high-shelf exposure must both increase. No cell may
reuse a track, and no afterimage may overlap the next spark.

### Euphoric-mosaic execution contract

`F353–F384` contains eight consecutive four-fragment stanzas at a fixed
130 BPM. Every stanza uses `4/8/4/8` source beats and alternates
`arrival/contradiction/arrival/contradiction`. Both arrivals must cite valence
of at least 0.60. Every contradiction must cite arousal of at most 0.45 or
wistful evidence of at least 0.35. Sources are formant-preserving
pitch-normalized to one stanza-local tonal anchor: either one Camelot code or
one relative-major/minor A/B pair. The anchor must change between stanzas so
the result cannot settle into a conventional key mix.
Tempo correction remains within ±6%. Cuts are dry and serial: no overlap, no
runtime choice, and no waiting for analysis during playback.

### Harmonic-implication execution contract

`F385–F400` contains eight dry two-fragment dyads at a fixed 128 BPM. Every
dyad uses a 4-beat tonal body followed by a 2-beat filtered ghost. The bodies
trace `8A → 8B → 9B → 10B → 10A → 11A → 12A → 1A`, beginning from the A-minor
residue left by F384. Each ghost exposes only enough pitch to anticipate the
next body through that code or its relative A/B pair; the final ghost exposes
1B, the relative-major seam beside 1A.

Each source is formant-preserving pitch-normalized to its written Camelot code.
Primary bodies require tonal confidence of at least 0.75. Ghosts use HP
1.8 kHz, no sub, and no tail, so they behave as connective overtones rather
than second songs. Pitch movement is at most six semitones and tempo correction
remains within ±6%. No dyad overlaps another. A dyad may be committed only when
both sources are analyzed and ready; playback may begin only after all eight
dyads and every later fragment are committed.

The route is Camelot-compatible, but four-beat bodies and two-beat ghosts deny
the listener enough time to hear a conventional key mix. Harmony is inferred
from succession, never sustained by a blend.

### Bright/dark contradiction execution contract

`F401–F416` contains eight dry two-fragment pairs at a fixed 126 BPM. Each pair
uses a 4-beat bright arrival followed by an 8-beat dark answer. Every arrival
must satisfy one arrival bundle: (a) brightness at least 0.48 and valence at
least 0.60; (b) brightness at least 0.48, valence from 0.55 through 0.60, energy
at least 0.80, and shelf at least +2.5 dB; or (c) brightness from 0.45 through
0.48, valence at least 0.60, energy at least 0.80, and shelf at least +3.0 dB.
Bundle (d) accepts brightness from 0.45 through 0.48, valence at least 0.55,
energy at least 0.80, arousal at least 0.58, and shelf at least +3.5 dB.
These bundles prevent one tightly centered classifier from becoming a blocking
control signal while retaining positive affect, force, and audible light. The
brightness bands are calibrated to the analyzed crate: nominally bright
material clusters around 0.46–0.50, so a single 0.52 gate would reject the
intended color rather than describe it. Its answer must cite darkness of at
least 0.55 and source brightness below
the arrival. A source drop of 0.07 or more may pass untreated. A smaller source
drop requires explicit rendered compensation: arrival shelf at least +2.5 dB,
answer shelf at most -3.0 dB, and answer low-pass no higher than 6.2 kHz. This
keeps analysis evidential rather than allowing a tightly centered classifier to
hold the composition loop. The darker evidence receives twice the duration, so
contradiction outlives the promise.

Odd pairs share vocal quality: cited vocal probability differs by at most 0.08
and both fragments receive the same intelligibility mask. Even pairs share
pitch: both sources are formant-preserving pitch-normalized to one Camelot code
or relative A/B pair, with tonal confidence of at least 0.75. Pitch movement
and tempo correction remain within ±6 semitones and ±6%. Cuts are serial and
dry, with no tail or runtime choice. A pair may be committed only after both
analyses are ready.

### Drum-removal execution contract

`F417–F432` contains four consecutive `1/2/4/8` source-beat cells at fixed
126 BPM. Within every cell, drum evidence and rendered transient exposure fall
in that order while consonant attacks lengthen into breath. Cell 1 moves from
full drums to no drum stem. Cell 2 permits no kick, then removes snare. Cell 3
begins with hats only and becomes percussion-free by its 4-beat fragment. Cell
4 contains no drum stem at all. Forward motion comes from pitched attacks,
amplitude envelopes, and dry breath gaps no longer than one eighth-note.

Every fragment names its allowed transient class: `full`, `no-kick`,
`hats-only`, `micro-percussion`, or `none`. Disallowed drum classes are removed
with stem masking and transient shaping, never by waiting for a quieter source.
Tempo correction remains within ±6%; cuts are serial and dry; all sixteen
analyses must be ready before playback.

The listener should understand the false sun only when it begins to disappear.

## Movement VI — Skin of the Screen

**Fragments:** `F433–F500`

Return to exposed material, but do not reproduce Movement I. The listener now
recognizes the machine inside every texture. Reduce the grid from 130 BPM to
free time through increasingly long gaps between short fragments.

### Phrase design

- `F433–F448`: machine fragments lose their low end and become tiny surfaces.
- `F449–F464`: human cuts. Use dry, low-confidence vocal evidence with up to
  35 ms of intentional timing imperfection.
- `F465–F480`: alternate one bright and one dark fragment, shortening each pair
  from 8 beats to 1 beat.
- `F481–F492`: twelve fragments form a decelerating exhale.
- `F493–F499`: seven one-beat details separated by expanding silence:
  `1/8, 1/8, 1/4, 1/4, 1/2, 1/2` beat.
- `F500`: a 4-beat fragment whose analysis is low in energy, low in rhythmic
  density, and neither maximally dark nor bright. Play it dry, centered, and
  unlooped. Cut before its source resolves.

### Resolved Movement VI duration lattice

The final 68 fragments consume exactly the remaining duration inventory:
`30 × 1`, `13 × 2`, `10 × 4`, `13 × 8`, and `2 × 16` score beats. Total
performance material is 232 grid beats. For cuts of eight beats or longer,
score beats also count synchronized source beats. Shorter transient-gated
cuts occupy destination-grid windows and may run natively inside those
windows; this avoids inventing source beats when tempo is unrelated.
Duration is fixed before source selection:

| Phrase | Fragments | Score-beat durations in score order |
|---|---:|---|
| machine surfaces | F433–F448 | `16, 8, 8, 8, 8, 4, 4, 2, 2, 2, 2, 1, 1, 1, 1, 1` |
| human cuts | F449–F464 | `8, 1, 4, 1, 8, 2, 1, 1, 8, 1, 2, 1, 1, 1, 1, 1` |
| bright/dark pairs | F465–F480 | `8, 8, 8, 8, 4, 4, 4, 4, 2, 2, 2, 2, 1, 1, 1, 1` |
| decelerating exhale | F481–F492 | `1, 1, 1, 1, 2, 2, 2, 4, 4, 8, 8, 16` |
| access details | F493–F499 | `1, 1, 1, 1, 1, 1, 1` |
| unresolved cut | F500 | `4` |

### Movement VI performance arc

**F433–F448 — machine surfaces.** Begin in F432's D-minor field at 130 BPM,
then descend through local grids
`130,130,129,129,128,128,127,127,126,126,125,125,124,123,122,122 BPM`.
The first 16-beat source
exposes a complete machine texture without reproducing Movement I; each
following cut removes low-frequency evidence, shortens the source window, and
moves its high-pass corner from 70 Hz toward 240 Hz. Energy descends from
approximately 0.70 to 0.42. The shared performance bus—not every source's
catalog key—traces `7A → 7B → 8B → 8A`: D minor remembers F major, falls
through C major, and arrives at A minor. Mask thirds or isolate noise,
transients, and fifths instead of forcing a source through a large
transposition.
Fragments F444–F448 are five different one-beat surfaces, never repetitions of
one transient.

**F449–F464 — human cuts.** Hold the grid between 128 and 124 BPM while machine
surfaces admit dry human evidence. Every source must have semantic vocal
probability between 0.18 and 0.58, but the selected window must end before a
complete lyric or phrase becomes intelligible. Alternate early and late
placement by `-18, +21, -27, +13, -35, +29, -11, +17, -24, +31, -16, +9,
-32, +26, -7, +19 ms`; these offsets are written edits, not runtime choices.
Keep formants natural and use no pitch shift beyond two semitones. Energy moves
from roughly 0.58 to 0.35. `8A → 9A → 10A` is the bus-color route: pitched
human fragments may state it, while unpitched consonants and breaths pass
without invented key labels.

**F465–F480 — bright/dark pairs.** Eight serial pairs alternate relative major
then minor. Pair durations are `8, 8, 4, 4, 2, 2, 1, 1` beats per fragment.
Walk clockwise around the Camelot wheel on the shared harmonic bed:
`7B/7A, 8B/8A, 9B/9A, 10B/10A, 11B/11A, 12B/12A, 1B/1A, 2B/2A`.
Source fragments need not state every target chord. A bright question may be
noise or percussion; a dark answer may be a filtered transient. Only audible
pitched material must agree with the local bed.
Within each pair, match energy within 0.08 and rhythmic density within 0.12;
brightness must cross 0.50 in opposite directions. No crossfade: each dark
answer begins on the exact dry edge of its bright question. Pair-average
energy descends from approximately 0.66 to 0.30.

**F481–F492 — decelerating exhale.** Source windows lengthen
`1,1,1,1,2,2,2,4,4,8,8,16` beats while attack density falls. Start near a
nominal 120 BPM; reduce the implied grid through source selection and written
breath gaps rather than extreme stretching. Require rhythmic density maxima of
`0.72, 0.68, 0.64, 0.60, 0.56, 0.52, 0.48, 0.43, 0.38, 0.33, 0.28, 0.23`.
Key confidence also declines; F489–F492 must favor dyads, drones, or masked
thirds over functional chords. The final 16-beat source loses pulse while
remaining audible—no fade and no silence substitution.

**F493–F499 — access details.** Use seven unique one-beat sources with one
recognizable physical detail each: breath, fingertip, relay click, bowed grain,
room reflection, cable noise, and screen-like whine. Separate them by the
written gaps `1/8, 1/8, 1/4, 1/4, 1/2, 1/2` beat. Gap growth is intentional and
is the only permitted silence longer than one eighth-note. Details descend in
energy toward 0.10; none may contain a complete kick, snare, word, or tonal
cadence.

**F500 — unresolved cut.** Select a four-beat source with energy `0.12–0.25`,
rhythmic density no higher than `0.25`, vocal probability below `0.20`, and
dark/bright balance inside `0.43–0.57`. Favor a D/A dyad with no audible third,
or remove the third in the edit. Play the source dry, centered, unlooped, and
without tail extension. Cut while its local energy slope is still non-zero,
before source harmony or ambience reaches rest.

### Movement VI evidence gates

- all 68 sources are unique, independently discovered in the closed 500-track
  crate, absent from F001–F432, and analyzed before their atomic row is
  written;
- cuts of eight source beats or longer may correct at most ±8%; low-density
  material with no audible pulse may run natively beneath the written grid;
- cuts of four score beats or fewer are transient-gated one-shots. Their
  source clock is evidence, not a synchronization obligation. They may run
  natively and hard-gate at the destination window, or correct by at most
  ±12%; no half/double-time relabeling is allowed;
- every cue range fits inside its cited analyzed segment and supplies enough
  source audio for its exact duration;
- adjacent rendered fragments may change normalized energy by at most `0.12`
  and brightness by at most `0.18`, except at the written F465 pair reset;
- bright/dark relations use local segment evidence. Harmonic labels describe
  audible pitched material or the shared bed, never an invented source chord;
- every human timing offset, gap, filter, pitch edit, and dry cut is resolved
  in the score; engine receives no musical decision during playback.

## Atomic score row

Every fragment in the final score uses this compact form:

```text
F### | score 00:00.000 | 4 beats @ 130
source TRACK_ID — Artist — Title
cue SEGMENT_ID 12.345–14.191s | source 129.8 BPM (0.98), 8B (0.81)
e .63 slope +.021 rhythm .84 vocal .08 valence .41 arousal .66 dark .57
role machine/answer | bus D | gain -5.0 dB | HP 45 Hz | LP open
enter hard on beat 3; decapitate previous A; no tail; hand transient to F###
```

Rows must be chronological and sufficient for execution. Prose such as “mix
smoothly,” “maintain the vibe,” or “transition when appropriate” is invalid.

## Commit checks

Before committing, verify all of the following:

- exactly 500 fragment rows and 500 unique track IDs;
- every cited analysis is ready and every segment/time range exists;
- duration distribution is exactly `180/140/100/60/20`;
- every fragment is 16 beats or shorter;
- movements contain `48/64/96/128/96/68` fragments;
- score times are monotonic after expansion;
- every hard cut has its next source ready before the cut;
- cuts of eight beats or longer stay within ±8%; shorter transient-gated cuts
  either run natively inside exact score windows or stay within ±12%; no
  half/double-time relabeling;
- no accidental full-band overlap;
- no accidental silence longer than one eighth-note;
- M5 occurs at its written formal positions;
- `F329` is the global energy peak;
- `F500` is a dry, unresolved ending;
- the score leaves no musical choice to make during playback.

If any check fails, repair the score before committing it. Do not begin
playback with a partial score.
